How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Autobiographical memories differ systematically with remoteness, yet little is known about the temporal form of these differences or when they are most pronounced. One consistent change over time concerns visual perspective-as memories become more remote, owneyes perspectives become less prominent and observer-like perspectives more prominent. Because visual perspective specifies the vantage point from which a remembered event is mentally seen, these shifts may reflect changes in how visual imagery of past events is reconstructed over time. We examined the temporal trajectory of visual perspective across 18,229 autobiographical memories from 80 participants, using memory age measured in days to characterize its relationship with own-eyes and observer-like perspectives at a finer temporal resolution than in previous work. For both perspectives, age-related differences followed a negatively accelerated trajectory. Observer perspective increased and own-eyes perspective decreased most sharply among relatively recent memories, with progressively smaller differences at greater memory ages. Approximately half of the total modeled difference occurred within the first four months, although the trajectory continued across several years. Higher reliving and emotional intensity were associated with attenuated age-related shifts in both perspectives, and rehearsal also moderated their trajectories. These findings show that visual perspective varies nonlinearly across autobiographical memory age and identify the early months after an event as a particularly dynamic period. More broadly, the results characterize the temporal organization of autobiographical imagery, linking the viewpoint from which past events are visualized to both their remoteness and the subjective qualities of recollection.

Introduction

Autobiographical memories (AMs) span remarkably long timescales, from events that occurred only hours ago to those remembered years later. Across these timescales, memories are not static, but can change in both their content and how they are experienced. More remote AMs tend to contain less episodic detail and are experienced with less vividness and emotional intensity [1] [2] [3] [4] , patterns that may partly reflect reduced accessibility or transformation of event-specific information over time 5, 6.

Yet considerably less is known about the temporal form of these differences in memories over time. Do characteristics of autobiographical remembering vary at a relatively constant rate across memory age, or are the largest differences concentrated relatively early after an event, followed by progressively smaller differences as memories become more remote? Characterizing this temporal trajectory is important for understanding when autobiographical remembering differs most strongly with remoteness and for identifying the retention intervals over which processes contributing to these differences are most likely to operate.

Visual perspective plays a generative role in autobiographical imagery by shaping how a remembered event is constructed and where the self is located within the imagined scene 7, 8 . Memories can be experienced from an own-eyes perspective, corresponding to the viewpoint from which memories are typically experienced from, and from an observer-like perspective, in which the rememberer could see themself from an external vantage point 9 . Own-eyes memories are generally associated with greater sensory detail, reliving, and emotional intensity than observer-like memories [9] [10] [11] , as well as richer reconstruction of scene-based information 12 . Importantly, visual perspective is not simply a passive correlate of memory quality. Experimentally altering the perspective adopted during retrieval can change the phenomenological experience and remembered content of an event and influence how that event is subsequently remembered [13] [14] [15] . Visual perspective therefore provides a window onto the constructive organization of autobiographical remembering.

Visual perspective also varies reliably with memory remoteness. Retrospective studies consistently show that relatively recent memories are more strongly associated with an own-eyes perspective, whereas more remote memories are increasingly associated with an observer-like perspective 3, 9, 11, 12 . Prospective studies further demonstrate that perspective can change within individual memories across repeated retrievals 4, 16 , although such changes can occur in both directions. What remains unclear is the functional form of the relationship between visual perspective and memory age.

Previous studies have typically compared broad temporal categories or followed relatively small numbers of memories across a limited number of retrieval occasions, making it difficult to determine whether age-related differences accumulate uniformly or are disproportionately concentrated at shorter retention intervals. Several findings suggest the latter possibility. Talarico and Rubin 4 observed changes across logarithmically spaced intervals of 7, 42, and 224 days, while Wardell et al. 16 detected perspective shifts across approximately 10 weeks. Reliable differences across these comparatively short timescales raise the possibility that the relationship between visual perspective and memory age is negatively accelerated, with the greatest differences occurring among relatively recent memories and progressively smaller differences at greater memory ages. Such a trajectory would parallel a broader property of memory retention. The temporal distribution and retention of autobiographical information are often characterized by negatively accelerated functions in which change is greatest over shorter intervals and slows with increasing time 5, [17] [18] [19] [20] . Although this literature has primarily concerned the accessibility, frequency, or retention of autobiographical information, a comparable temporal form in visual perspective would suggest that the phenomenological organization of remembering also varies most strongly over relatively short retention intervals. Perspective may be particularly informative in this respect because maintaining an own-eyes viewpoint may depend on access to perceptual and spatial information specifying the original organization of the event, whereas reconstruction from an observer-like viewpoint does not preserve the originally experienced vantage point. Determining whether visual perspective follows a similar negatively accelerated relationship with memory age can therefore help establish whether this aspect of autobiographical reconstruction varies over a timescale comparable to other forms of memory change.

The strength of the relationship between memory age and visual perspective may also depend on characteristics of the remembered event. Reliving and emotional intensity are closely associated with visual perspective, with memories experienced with stronger reliving and emotion more likely to be remembered from an own-eyes perspective [21] [22] [23] . Some evidence also suggests that these characteristics may be more stable for phenomenologically stronger memories. For example, Talarico and Rubin 4 , found that flashbulb memories remained relatively stable in perspective across retention intervals, whereas everyday memories from a similar period were more likely to shift toward an observer-like perspective. Rehearsal may likewise be relevant because repeated retrieval can influence the perspective and episodic information available during subsequent remembering 13 . Thus, the temporal relationship between memory age and visual perspective may vary according to the extent to which a memory continues to be strongly relived, emotionally intense, or repeatedly retrieved.

The current study examined the relationship between memory age and visual perspective using a large retrospective dataset of AMs spanning the preceding five years 15, 24 . Eighty participants generated more than 18,000 memories-over 200 per participant-and provided dates and separate ratings of own-eyes and observer perspective, yielding dense and continuous sampling across memory age. Our primary aim was to determine the functional form of the relationship between memory age and visual perspective. We compared linear and nonlinear models and used generalized additive mixed models (GAMMs) 25 to characterize flexibly when age-related differences were most pronounced across the retention interval. We predicted a negatively accelerated trajectory, such that observer perspective would increase and own-eyes perspective would decrease most sharply among relatively recent memories, with progressively smaller differences at greater memory ages. As a secondary aim, we examined whether reliving, emotional intensity, and rehearsal moderated these relationships, testing whether the temporal trajectory varied as a function of the phenomenological strength and retrieval history of the remembered event.

Results

The two datasets did not differ significantly in memory age or any of the measured memory characteristics, and the pattern of intercorrelations was highly similar across studies (Supplementary Tables S2-S3 and Figure S1-S2). We therefore combined the datasets for subsequent analyses.

Memory Age is Associated with Shifts in Visual Perspective.

We first examined whether visual perspective varied as a function of memory age. For each perspective rating, we compared linear, logarithmic, and quadratic specifications of memory age (see Supplementary Figure S3). Memory age significantly predicted both observer and own-eyes perspective ratings across all three models. For observer ratings, perspective increased with memory age in the linear model (β = 0.055, SE = .007, t(81) = 8.78), logarithmic model (β = 0.166, SE = 0.019, t(79) = 8.54), and quadratic model (β = 32.84, SE = 3.75, t(80) = 8.76). Conversely, own-eyes ratings decreased with memory age in the linear model (β = -0.06, SE = 0.006, t(77) = -10.5), logarithmic model (β = -0.181, SE = 0.018, t(79) = -9.98), and quadratic model (β = -35.68, SE = 3.43, t(77) = -10.41).

Model comparisons indicated that the logarithmic model provided the best fit for both perspectives (see Table 1 and Tables S4-S5), with lower AIC values than either the linear model (observer: ΔAIC = 97.54; own-eyes: ΔAIC = 119.68) or the quadratic model (observer: ΔAIC = 66.64; own-eyes: ΔAIC = 80.09). As shown in Figure 1, observer ratings increased with memory age whereas own-eyes ratings decreased, with both trajectories showing a steeper change at shorter memory ages followed by a progressively shallower rate of change as memories became more remote. This pattern is consistent with a negatively accelerated relationship between memory age and visual perspective. Logarithmic Log(Memory Age) 66,040.63 66,087.49 Polynomial (Quadratic) Memory Age + (Memory Age)² 66,107.27 66,161.94 Own-Eyes Linear Memory Age 63,952.03 63,998.90 Logarithmic Log(Memory Age) 63,832.35 63,879.22 Polynomial (Quadratic) Memory Age + (Memory Age)² 63,912.44 63,967.11 Note. AIC = Akaike Information Criterion; BIC = Bayesian Information Criterion. The winning model (lowest AIC and BIC) is shown in bold. Memory Age was centered; Log(Memory Age) is the centered logtransformed memory age. Despite these systematic associations with memory age, the variance explained by the fixed effects was modest. For observer ratings, marginal R² was .020, compared with a conditional R² of .355 when participant-level random effects were included. A similar pattern emerged for own-eyes ratings, with a marginal R² of .026 and a conditional R² of .375. Thus, although memory age was systematically associated with shifts in perspective, substantial variability was attributable to individual differences in the overall tendency to remember events from particular perspectives.

The power function model showed that the power exponent was significant for both observer (b = 0.062, SE = 0.007, p < .001) and own-eyes ratings (b = -0.044, SE = 0.005, p < .001). In both cases, the estimated exponents were consistent with a negatively accelerated trajectory in which the rate of change diminished with increasing memory age. The marginal R² for the power model was .020 for observer and .021 owneyes ratings, compared with .020 and .026, respectively, for the logarithmic models.

Although these values should not be interpreted as formal model comparisons because of the different outcome scales, the power-function analyses converged with the primary analyses in supporting a nonlinear, decelerating relationship between memory age and visual perspective. The results were consistent when the two studies were modeled separately (Table S6) and when memories in the earliest time point were excluded (Table S7).

In summary, memory age predicted systematic shifts in both perspectives, with observer ratings increasing and own-eyes ratings decreasing as memories became more remote. Among the directly comparable models, the logarithmic function provided the best fit, while the power-function analyses provided converging evidence for the broader negatively accelerated trajectory characterized by greater change at shorter memory ages and progressively slower change over time.

Memory Qualities and Perspective Change Across Memory Age.

We next examined whether emotional intensity, reliving, and rehearsal moderated the changes in visual perspective over time. Across both perspectives, higher within-person reliving and emotional intensity were associated with lower observer ratings and higher owneyes ratings (Reliving: observer β = -0.122, p < .001, own-eyes β = 0.245, p < .001;

Emotional Intensity: observer β = -0.044, p = .029, own-eyes β = 0.150, p < .001; see

Figure 2). Both characteristics also showed significant interactions with log(memory age) for both perspectives, indicating that the shift in perspective across memory age was weaker for memories rated higher in reliving and emotional intensity (Reliving: observer β = -0.016, p = .002, own-eyes β = 0.018, p < .001; Emotional intensity: observer β = -0.013, p = .008, own-eyes β = 0.018, p < .001).

Rehearsal, D. Own-Eyes × Reliving, E. Own-Eyes × Intensity, F. Own-Eyes × Rehearsal. Shaded bands represent 95% confidence intervals.

In contrast, within-person rehearsal showed different patterns across perspectives. In the own-eyes model, rehearsal predicted higher own-eyes ratings (β = 0.130, p < .001) and a significant interaction with memory age (β = 0.016, p = .003). In the observer model, the within-person rehearsal coefficient was not significant at the reference memory age (β = -0.034, p = .087), but its interaction with log memory age was negative and significant (β = -0.012, p = .035), indicating that the association between rehearsal and observer ratings became more negative as memory age increased. Between-person differences in reliving, rehearsal, and emotional intensity were not significantly associated with either perspective (all ps > .33; see Table S8 for full statistics).

Time Course of Shifts in Visual Perspective.

The preceding model comparisons indicated that changes in visual perspective were better characterized by a negatively accelerated than a linear trajectory. To characterize the timing of these changes more flexibly, we next fit GAMMs, which allowed the relationship between memory age and perspective to vary nonlinearly without imposing a specific functional form. The GAMMs indicated significant nonlinear associations between memory age and both perspective ratings. Observer ratings increased most rapidly among relatively recent memories, followed by a progressively slower rate of increase as memories became more remote (edf = 3.46, F = 662.7, p < .001). Own-eyes ratings showed the complementary pattern, decreasing most rapidly among relatively recent memories and more gradually for more remote memories (edf = 4.47, F = 940.4, p < .001).

To characterize the time course of perspective change, we used the log-scale GAMM to estimate when each perspective reached 25%, 50%, 75%, and 90% of its total modeled change. Both perspectives changed most rapidly at the earliest observed memory age, with the rate of change gradually slowing thereafter. This pattern was consistent with a logarithmic trajectory without an interior peak (Table 2 and Figure 3; the same milestones are shown on the raw rating scale in Figure S4).

The timing of the milestones differed somewhat by perspective. Own-eyes reached 25% of its total change sooner than observer (approximately 5 vs. 10 days), while the two perspectives reached 50% at nearly the same time (117 vs. 118 days). At the later milestones, observer reached each sooner than own-eyes: 75% at approximately 584 vs. 650 days (19.2 vs. 21.4 months) and 90% at 1,164 vs. 1,213 days (38.3 vs. 39.9 months). The estimated rate of change at the earliest observed memory age was also much higher than at 30 days: approximately 51 times higher for observer and 97 times higher for own-eyes.

Direct Comparison of Change Rates Across Perspectives.

Although the milestone analysis suggested small timing differences between the two perspectives, it did not directly test whether their rates of change differed. To compare rates directly, own-eyes ratings were reversed onto the same scale direction as observer ratings (8own-eyes), and a linear mixed model was fit with perspective, log(memory age), and their interaction. The interaction was not significant (β = 0.011, SE = 0.010, t(34,813) = 1.11, p = .269). The estimated slopes were similar for observer (β = 0.165, SE = 0.007) and reversed own-eyes (β = 0.176, SE = 0.007), providing no evidence that their linear rates of change over log memory age differed.

A supplementary GAMM analysis allowing the rate of change for each perspective to vary across memory age produced the same conclusion (Supplementary Methods). The simultaneous 95% confidence band for the difference between the perspective-specific derivatives included zero across the entire interval, providing no evidence that observer and own-eyes perspectives differed in their rate of change (Figure S5).

Discussion

The present findings provide new insight into how visual perspective changes as AMs become more remote. Consistent with previous research, observer-like perspectives increased and own-eyes perspectives decreased as memories became more remote 4, 9, 16 . Critically, these shifts in perspective did not occur at a constant rate.

Both perspectives followed a rapid early change followed by progressively slower change. Additional analyses characterizing the temporal trajectory further indicated that changes in perspective were concentrated among the most recent memories, and then gradually slowed without fully leveling off within the five-year range. Finally, the relationship between memory age and visual perspective varied with other characteristics of the memory. Higher reliving and emotional intensity attenuated the shift in both perspective over time. Rehearsal also moderated the relationship between shifts in perspective and memory age for own-eyes perspectives, such that the decline in own-eyes ratings with increasing memory age was less pronounced for more frequently rehearsed memories. Overall, these findings show that visual perspective changes systematically as memories become more remote, while revealing that the trajectory of this change is dynamic: it is most pronounced early in a memory's lifespan and varies with the phenomenological qualities and rehearsal history of the remembered event.

Prior research has established that visual perspective varies with memory remoteness, but has generally not been designed to characterize the functional form of this relationship. Using dense sampling of AMs within participants, we show that changes in visual perspective follow a negatively accelerated trajectory, with the greatest differences among relatively recent memories and progressively smaller changes as memories become more remote. Among directly comparable candidate models, the logarithmic function provided the best fit, while a power-function analysis showed a similar pattern of rapid early change followed by progressively slower change.

This convergence is theoretically informative because a similar temporal pattern characterizes forgetting, with the greatest loss of memory information occurring over shorter retention intervals and progressively smaller losses thereafter 20, 26 . One possibility, therefore, is that perspective shifts are related to declining availability of event-specific information that supports the reconstruction of an event from its original vantage point. As perceptual and spatial information from the original experience becomes less accessible, remembering the event from an own-eyes perspective may become more difficult, increasing the likelihood that it is reconstructed from an observerlike viewpoint. In this sense, the shift toward observer perspective may be one phenomenological manifestation of broader transformations in how autobiographical events are represented as they become more remote.

A broader possibility is that changes in visual perspective reflect transformations in AM representations as they become more remote. According to memory transformation accounts, richly detailed episodic representations may become increasingly more like schematic or generalized representations over time 6 . From this perspective, own-eyes remembering may be more readily supported when the original perceptual and spatial organization of an event can be reinstated, whereas observerlike remembering may become more likely as reconstruction depends less strongly on the original viewpoint. Interestingly, neuroimaging evidence also indicates that the neural representations supporting AM continue to change substantially over this period.

Barry et al. 27 , for example, found that memory specific representations in the ventromedial prefrontal cortex changed nonmonotonically with memory age, increasing towards a peak at approximately 8-12 months before declining and later re-emerging for more remote memories. Although these neural changes cannot be directly equated with the changes in visual perspective, they demonstrate that AM representations undergo substantial and protracted reorganization as memories age. The present behavioral trajectory showed a similarly extended time course: roughly half of the predicted perspective shift had occurred within the first few months, but change continued thereafter and did not approach its slowest phase until several years later.

Thus, rather than marking a discrete transition from recent to remote memory, visual perspective may change alongside the broader, gradual transformation of AM representations over time. Visual perspective may therefore provide a phenomenological indicator of changes in how AMs are represented with increasing remoteness.

By characterizing the temporal trajectory our findings also provide new insight into when shifts in visual perspective are most likely to occur. The greatest estimated rates of change were observed among the most recent memories, followed by progressively smaller changes as memories became more remote. This temporal pattern helps place previous findings in context. Prospective studies examining memories across relatively short retention intervals (e.g., 16 ), may disproportionately capture the period during which perspective is changing most rapidly. Indeed, the estimated rate of change declined sharply within the first month, suggesting that studies beginning even a month or two after an event may miss much of this early change.

Conversely, retrospective studies comparing memories across much broader intervals (e.g., 11, 28 ) may reflect differences that emerged disproportionately during the earlier months rather than change accumulating uniformly across the entire retention interval.

The present findings therefore help narrow the temporal window in which future longitudinal studies could most directly examine the processes that predict whether an AM maintains its original perspective or shifts toward an observer-like viewpoint.

The relationship between memory age and visual perspective also varied with the phenomenological qualities of the memory. Higher reliving and emotional intensity attenuated the relationship between memory age and both perspectives: the positive association between memory age and observer perspective and the negative association between memory age and own-eyes perspective were both weaker for memories rated higher on these qualities. This pattern is consistent with Talarico and Rubin 4 , who found that visual perspective remained relatively stable for flashbulb memories while everyday memories showed a greater shift toward an observer perspective over time. If perspective shifts partly reflect reduced access to eventspecific information, memories that retain a stronger sense of reliving and emotional intensity may also retain more of the perceptual and spatial information that supports reconstruct from the original viewpoint. In this sense, the moderation findings are consistent with the possibility that phenomenologically richer memories are relatively resistant to the changes in perspective that accompany increasing remoteness. This interpretation is also broadly compatible with accounts in which richly recollected, eventspecific memories continue to rely on hippocampally supported representations 29.

Emotional intensity may be related to perspective change through an additional route.

Visual perspective can itself influence the emotional experience of autobiographical remembering, with observer-like perspectives often associated with reduced affect 30.

The weaker perspective shifts observed for more emotionally intense memories may therefore reflect the close coupling between visual perspective and emotional experience, in addition to the persistence of event-specific information. Although these interaction effects were small, their consistency across both perspective measures suggests that the temporal trajectory of perspective change varies systematically across AMs.

Rehearsal showed a somewhat different pattern. More frequently rehearsed memories showed a weaker decline in own-eyes perspective with increasing memory age, and own-eyes ratings were also higher overall. This pattern raises the possibility that repeated retrieval helps maintain access to aspects of an event that support remembering from the original vantage point. Consistent with this possibility, experimental work shows that the perspective adopted during retrieval can alter the episodic information that remains accessible on subsequent retrievals 13, 31 . Repeatedly retrieving a memory from an own-eyes perspective may therefore help preserve information that supports that perspective, although this interpretation requires direct longitudinal testing. More broadly, the perspective-specific association with rehearsal is compatible with evidence that own-eyes and observer perspectives are dissociable rather than mutually exclusive 11 , suggesting that they may share some retrieval dynamics while remaining differentially sensitive to particular memory characteristics.

The present findings also provide specific guidance for future longitudinal research. Following the same memories across time will be important for determining whether changes in visual perspective accompany, precede, or follow changes in other features of autobiographical remembering. The temporal trajectory found here suggests that sampling should be densest during the earliest retention intervals, when perspective change appears to be most pronounced. Combining such longitudinal designs with neuroimaging would further allow researchers to test whether shifts in visual perspective correspond to changes in the neural systems supporting AM retrieval 8 . This approach could directly test whether visual perspective serves as a phenomenological marker of broader representational transformation in AM over time.

Several limitations should be acknowledged. First, the retrospective design does not allow direct observation of changes in perspective within the same memories over time. Differences across memory ages may therefore reflect both within-memory change and differences in which memories are retained or sampled at different retention intervals. Memory age was also estimated retrospectively and may be subject to dating error 32, 33 . Nevertheless, longitudinal research demonstrates that visual perspective can change within individual memories over time 4 . Importantly, such shifts can occur in both directions over relatively short intervals 16 , indicating that the age-related trajectory observed here should not be interpreted as a uniform progression from own-eyes to observer perspective within every memory. Second, because all phenomenological characteristics were assessed concurrently with visual perspective at retrieval, the causal direction of their associations with perspective cannot be determined. These characteristics may influence perspective change, result from changes in perspective, or reflect other properties of the memory. Third, the present study was restricted to memories from the preceding five years, limiting conclusions about whether similar trajectories extend to more remote AMs.

Conclusion.

The present study provides the first characterization of the trajectory of visual perspective change in AMs using dense sampling across continuous memory age. Visual perspective changed systematically with increasing remoteness, with the most rapid changes occurring shortly after an event and slowing thereafter.

This temporal pattern is broadly consistent with accounts proposing substantial reorganization of memory representations during the earlier stages of memory transformation 6, 27 . The trajectory also varied with the phenomenological qualities of the remembered event, such that memories associated with greater reliving and emotional intensity showed less pronounced perspective change whereas rehearsal slowed the rate of change in both perspectives but altered the overall level only for own-eyes ratings. More broadly, these findings highlight visual perspective as a potentially valuable behavioral marker of memory transformation, linking changes in the subjective experience of remembering with broader changes in how AMs are represented over time.

Methods

Participants

The data were drawn from two previously conducted studies that employed identical procedures 15, 24 . The combined sample consisted of 80 (40 from each study) young adults aged between 18 and 30 years. (n = 46 female; Mage = 21.6 years, SD = 3.19), who reported no history of neurological or psychiatric episodes or current use of medication.

Procedure

The study was conducted in person. After providing informed consent, participants completed an AM retrieval task. First, they were provided with a list of possible events (e.g., camping, going on a date) to help generate AMs. The recalled memories did not need to be personally significant but had to involve a clearly identifiable episode that was experienced at a specific time and place. All memories were required to have occurred within the past five years and to include events in which participants were personally involved. Each participant was asked to generate 228 24 or 240 15 memories. For each memory, participants provided a unique title and an event date. Participants reported the event date in month/day/year format as accurately as possible. When the exact day was unknown, participants were instructed to estimate the date by indicating the beginning (day 1), middle (day 15), or end (day 28) of the month.

Additionally, they were asked to rate their subjective experience related to each memory on the following four characteristics (based on 34 ):

Visual Perspective. Participants rated the extent to which they remembered the event from an own-eyes perspective and from an observer perspective, using separate scales (1 = not at all,7 = completely). Before providing these ratings, participants were given definitions of both perspectives, explaining that own-eyes memories are experienced from the original viewpoint of the self, whereas observer memories involve viewing the event from an external vantage point in which the self can be seen. Participants were informed that memories could be experienced exclusively from one perspective or fluctuate between perspectives.

Reliving.

Participants rated the extent to which they experienced a sense of reliving the event (1 = not at all,7 = as clearly as if it were happening now), reflecting the subjective feeling of reexperiencing the event again.

Emotional Intensity.

Participants rated the intensity of the emotion arousal associated with the memory (1 = none,7 = high), regardless of whether the emotion was positive or negative.

Rehearsal Frequency.

Participants rated how often they had thought or talked about the event since it occurred (1 = not at all,7 = more than any other memory).

There was an additional emotional valence rating in the St. Jacques et al., 24 study, however, we focused only on ratings common in both studies. It took participants approximately 5 hours to generate the memories and to rate and date them. Aggregate data from a subset of these memories, shown during a later fMRI session, are reported in St. Jacques et al. 15 and St. Jacques et al. 24.

Statistics and Reproducibility

Data were analyzed using R 35 within RStudio 36 using linear mixed-effects models to account for the hierarchical structure of memories nested within participants.

Separate models were conducted for observer and own-eyes ratings. Statistical significance was assessed at α = .05.

Use of Generative AI

ChatGPT (OpenAI) was used to assist with manuscript preparation, including improving the clarity and organization of text, refining scientific language, and discussing the presentation of statistical analyses. All analytical decisions, scientific interpretations, and final manuscript content were reviewed and approved by the authors, who retain full responsibility for the work.

Functional form of the relationship between memory age and visual perspective.

To characterize the functional form of the relationship between memory age and visual perspective, we fitted separate linear mixed-effects models for observer and own-eyes ratings. We compared linear, logarithmic, and quadratic specifications of memory age, with participant-level random intercepts and slopes. Linear and quadratic memory age terms were rescaled by dividing by 100 to facilitate model estimation.

Models were compared using AIC and BIC. We additionally fitted power functions as a convergent test of a negatively accelerated relationship. Because these models used log-transformed perspective ratings, their AIC and BIC values were not directly comparable with models fitted to the original rating scale; marginal R² and the estimated exponent were therefore used descriptively. Intercept-only models confirmed substantial between-person variability for both observer (ICC = .316) and own-eyes (ICC = .336) ratings, justifying the use of multilevel modeling for all analyses.

Moderation by memory characteristics.

The best-fitting model was chosen to investigate whether memory qualities moderate the relationship between memory age and perspective. Prior to modeling each characteristic was decomposed into within-and between-person components. While the within-person component was calculated as the memory-level deviation from each participant's own mean rating, the between-person component was calculated as each participant's own rating relative to the sample mean.

This approach allows us to distinguish memory to memory fluctuations from individual differences. Then, to examine whether memory qualities moderated changes in visual perspective over time, separate linear mixed-effects models were fit for observer and own-eyes ratings and for each memory quality (emotional intensity, reliving, and rehearsal). Those models predict each perspective rating from the within-person component, between-person component, and the interaction between the within-person component and Log(Memory Age), with a random intercept for participants and random slopes for Log(Memory Age) and the within-person component.

Temporal trajectory of visual perspective.

To characterize the temporal trajectory without imposing a particular parametric form, we fitted GAMMs 25 predicting each perspective rating from a smooth function of log(memory age), with participantlevel random effects. Models were restricted to the common memory-age range represented in both datasets (0-1820 days). Derivatives of the fitted smooths were used to characterize changes in slope across memory age and to estimate when 25%, 50%, 75%, and 90% of the total modeled difference across the five-year interval had been reached. The GAMMs were fit with selection (select = TRUE) and an increased smoothing penalty (gamma = 1.2) to enforce smooth, non-overfit functions and obtain highly reliable estimates of the rate of change. Model diagnostics confirmed an adequate basis dimension for the smooth term in both perspectives, with non-significant k-index tests (Observer: k-index = 1.01, p = .49; Own-Eyes: k-index = 1.00, p = .41).

Preliminary Analysis and Study Comparison.

Prior to the main analyses, we assessed the comparability of the two studies. We first filtered the data to exclude trials where memory age was missing or exceeded 5 years (132 excluded trials in total), and logtransformed memory age for subsequent modeling (Memory Age variable was adjusted to 0.5 days where the original value was 0 to allow for log transformation). The final dataset included 18,229 memory trials. The distribution of memory ages across theoretically meaningful time bins is presented in Supplementary Table S1.

Independent samples t-tests were conducted on the subject-level mean scores for memory age, observer perspective, own-eyes perspective, reliving, emotional intensity and rehearsal to test for significant differences between the two studies. No significant differences were found between the two study samples on any of the compared variables (memory age, observer, own-eyes perspective, reliving, emotional intensity, rehearsal, see Supplementary Table S2 and Supplementary Figure S1). These results indicate that, at the subject level, the memories sampled from both studies were statistically equivalent in terms of age and memory characteristics.

Correlations among all key variables in the combined dataset were computed. As expected, own-eyes and observer ratings showed a strong negative correlation (r = -.84).

Reliving and emotional intensity were positively associated with own-eyes and negatively associated with observer. Notably, the correlation between Rehearsal and Memory Age was negligible (r = -.03; see Supplementary Table S3). Furthermore, a Mantel test, which assesses the similarity between correlation matrices between two studies, revealed a very high and significant correlation between the intercorrelations of the memory characteristics (own-eyes, observer, reliving, rehearsal, emotional intensity, memory age) in the two studies (r = .969, p = .001; see Supplementary Figure S2). This suggests that the internal structure of the measured memory characteristics is highly similar across the two independent samples. Therefore, the data from the two studies were combined for subsequent modeling.

GAMM Analysis for Comparison of Change Rates Across Perspectives.

To evaluate whether the rate of change in observer and own-eyes (reversed) ratings differed as a function of memory age, we fit a generalized additive mixed model (GAMM) allowing each perspective's trajectory to vary independently (via perspective-specific smooth terms) across logtransformed, centered memory age, with a random intercept for participant. The first derivative (i.e., instantaneous rate of change) of each perspective-specific smooth was extracted at 200 evenly spaced points across the memory-age range, and a simultaneous 95% confidence interval was constructed for the difference between the two derivative curves at each point.

Model Comparison: Observer and Own-Eyes Perspectives

Days and Months to Reach Each Percentage of Total Predicted Change by PerspectiveNote. Days and months represent the memory age at which each perspective's model-predicted trajectory reached the indicated percentage of its total change across the full observed range (0 to 1,820 days), estimated from the log-scale GAMM.

Information Nilay Özdemir Haksever & Peggy L. St. Jacques* Department of Psychology, University of Alberta, Edmonton, Canada Supplementary Results

Distribution of Observer and Own-eyes Ratings by Study. Boxplots display the distribution of Observer (left) and Own-eyes (right) perspective ratings for Study 1 (St. Jacques et al., 2018) and Study 2 (St. Jacques et al., 2017). No study differences were observed on either variable. Supplementary Figure S2. Correlation Matrices by Study (Mantel Test). Heatmaps display Pearson correlation matrices for all measured variables separately for Study 1 (St. Jacques et al., 2018) and Study 2 (St. Jacques et al., 2017). Color indicates correlation direction and strength (blue = negative, red = positive). The Mantel test confirmed high structural similarity between matrices. Supplementary Figure S3. Comparison of Model Fits: Linear, Logarithmic, and Quadratic. Lines represent model-predicted values for each functional form for Observer (A) and Own-eyes (B) perspective ratings as a function of memory age. The logarithmic model (green) provided the best fit across both perspectives.Supplementary FigureS4. Time Course of Memory Perspective Shifts Estimated by GAMM. Smooth curves represent GAMM-estimated trajectories for Observer (blue) and Own-Eyes (orange) perspective ratings across memory age (days), on the raw rating scale. Dotted vertical lines mark the memory age at which each perspective reached 50% of its total predicted change (Observer: about 118 days; Own-Eyes: about 117 days). Dashed vertical lines mark the 90% milestone (Observer: about 1,164 days, 38.3 months; Own-Eyes: about 1,213 days, 39.9 months). The analysis was restricted to the common memory age range shared by both studies.
